I’m a designer, so details matter, obviously, and clients can be really particular—like, sometimes they’ll notice a spacing thing you’d swear no one could see.\n\nBut even when I look and it seems fine, and I know it’s probably fine, my mind just keeps circling back to it. Like, “What if there’s something I missed?” or “What if that wasn’t the final file?” It’s not huge, I guess, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about potential mistakes come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… they do feel like they just pop in whether I want them to or not. I’ll have already looked at the layout and think, okay, that’s probably fine, and then my brain kind of loops back like, “What if there’s one tiny spacing mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?”\n\nIt’s annoying because with design you’re trained to notice small things—like kerning and alignment and all that, and I once had a client comment on something really microscopic—so part of me goes, well, maybe it’s reasonable. But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing to think about it. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts about potential mistakes cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, “significant” sounds dramatic, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything. But it’s distressing in that it keeps pulling at me after I’ve already checked something and can see, logically, it’s probably fine.\n\nLike with a layout, I’ll think, okay, the spacing looks right, the file seems correct… and then I’m making coffee or answering another email and suddenly I’m back on, “What if I missed some tiny mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?” It’s this nagging feeling that won’t quite leave me alone. And then I end up annoyed with myself, because in design you’re supposed to be careful—but there’s careful, and then there’s my brain just circling the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work or taking specific actions to try and alleviate these nagging thoughts about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like, I don’t spend hours and hours on it, but before I submit something now I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it… sometimes more than once.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the whole design, like, “Why did I choose that spacing, is that aligned, does that crop make sense,” which—I mean, some of that is just part of being a designer. I had a professor in college who was obsessed with grids, actually, and I still hear her voice sometimes when I’m checking margins. But lately it feels less like normal review and more like I’m doing it to calm that nagging feeling.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it definitely slows me down. And I feel like it makes me less… loose creatively, I guess. Like I can’t just finish and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking your work repeatedly because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. It’s not like I think something catastrophic is going to happen, exactly, but I’m trying to prevent that feeling of, “Oh no, I missed something,” or sending the wrong version and then looking careless.\n\nSo before I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds funny, because designers are supposed to review things—I mean, I had this one art director who would catch the tiniest alignment issues, like almost invisible—but with me lately it’s more like I’m doing it so I can feel safe enough to hit send.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just trust that it’s done and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked your work repeatedly?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of the annoying part—it does help for a minute. Like if I re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it, then I feel like, okay, I can send this now.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll still mentally go back through every choice—spacing, crop, alignment, all that. It’s funny, because in design school we were basically trained to be obsessive about details, and I used to think that was just professionalism. But now it feels more like I’m checking so I can quiet myself down.\n\nIt doesn’t eat up my whole day, but it definitely makes me slower. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I remember in college we’d have these critiques where people would spend twenty minutes talking about one line being slightly off, and part of me still hears that voice, I guess.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more that I need it to feel exactly as it should be. There is some fear of a mistake, sure, but it’s not like I’m imagining some disaster every time.

It’s more… before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then I might mentally walk through the design choices one more time. Which sounds so small when I say it, but it adds friction. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it makes me slower and less loose creatively.

Sometimes I miss when I could just look at something and think, “Okay, that works,” and send it. Now it has to feel settled first.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so I’m here because I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I’ve missed some tiny mistake in a layout, or that I sent the wrong version to a client. I’m a designer, so details matter, obviously, and clients can be really particular—like, sometimes they’ll notice a spacing thing you’d swear no one could see.\n\nBut even when I look and it seems fine, and I know it’s probably fine, my mind just keeps circling back to it. Like, “What if there’s something I missed?” or “What if that wasn’t the final file?” It’s not huge, I guess, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about potential mistakes come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… they do feel like they just pop in whether I want them to or not. I’ll have already looked at the layout and think, okay, that’s probably fine, and then my brain kind of loops back like, “What if there’s one tiny spacing mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?”\n\nIt’s annoying because with design you’re trained to notice small things—like kerning and alignment and all that, and I once had a client comment on something really microscopic—so part of me goes, well, maybe it’s reasonable. But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing to think about it. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts about potential mistakes cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, “significant” sounds dramatic, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything. But it’s distressing in that it keeps pulling at me after I’ve already checked something and can see, logically, it’s probably fine.\n\nLike with a layout, I’ll think, okay, the spacing looks right, the file seems correct… and then I’m making coffee or answering another email and suddenly I’m back on, “What if I missed some tiny mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?” It’s this nagging feeling that won’t quite leave me alone. And then I end up annoyed with myself, because in design you’re supposed to be careful—but there’s careful, and then there’s my brain just circling the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work or taking specific actions to try and alleviate these nagging thoughts about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like, I don’t spend hours and hours on it, but before I submit something now I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it… sometimes more than once.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the whole design, like, “Why did I choose that spacing, is that aligned, does that crop make sense,” which—I mean, some of that is just part of being a designer. I had a professor in college who was obsessed with grids, actually, and I still hear her voice sometimes when I’m checking margins. But lately it feels less like normal review and more like I’m doing it to calm that nagging feeling.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it definitely slows me down. And I feel like it makes me less… loose creatively, I guess. Like I can’t just finish and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking your work repeatedly because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. It’s not like I think something catastrophic is going to happen, exactly, but I’m trying to prevent that feeling of, “Oh no, I missed something,” or sending the wrong version and then looking careless.\n\nSo before I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds funny, because designers are supposed to review things—I mean, I had this one art director who would catch the tiniest alignment issues, like almost invisible—but with me lately it’s more like I’m doing it so I can feel safe enough to hit send.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just trust that it’s done and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked your work repeatedly?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of the annoying part—it does help for a minute. Like if I re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it, then I feel like, okay, I can send this now.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll still mentally go back through every choice—spacing, crop, alignment, all that. It’s funny, because in design school we were basically trained to be obsessive about details, and I used to think that was just professionalism. But now it feels more like I’m checking so I can quiet myself down.\n\nIt doesn’t eat up my whole day, but it definitely makes me slower. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I mean, sometimes I’ll remember some old critique from design school about spacing or crops, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of just submitting the thing. But no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down enough that I notice it, and I feel less loose creatively, if that makes sense. Like if I weren’t so tense about how it might land, I could probably let go sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your work repeatedly if you knew that making a small mistake would not have significant consequences?\n\nPatient: I think maybe the urge would still be there, just… less intense? It’s not like I think something catastrophic is going to happen, exactly, but I’m trying to prevent that feeling of, “Oh no, I missed something,” or sending the wrong version and then looking careless.\n\nSo before I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds funny, because designers are supposed to review things—I mean, I had this one art director who would catch the tiniest alignment issues, like almost invisible—but with me lately it’s more like I’m doing it so I can feel safe enough to hit send.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just trust that it’s done and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked your work repeatedly?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of the annoying part—it does help for a minute. Like if I re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it, then I feel like, okay, I can send this now.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll still mentally go back through every choice—spacing, crop, alignment, all that. It’s funny, because in design school we were basically trained to be obsessive about details, and I used to think that was just professionalism. But now it feels more like I’m checking so I can quiet myself down.\n\nIt doesn’t eat up my whole day, but it definitely makes me slower. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I mean, sometimes I’ll remember some old critique from design school about spacing or crops, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of just submitting the thing. But no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down enough that I notice it, and I feel less loose creatively, if that makes sense. Like if I weren’t so tense about how it might land, I could probably let go sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your work repeatedly if you knew that making a small mistake would not have significant consequences?\n\nPatient: I think maybe the urge would still be there, just… less intense? Like even if someone told me, “A small mistake won’t matter,” I’d probably still want to re-open the file and compare the layers and re-save it, because that’s become this little ritual before I submit anything.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this thing where I mentally walk through the design choices again — which is annoying, because design is supposed to have some looseness to it. I remember in college we’d have these critiques where people would spend twenty minutes talking about one line being slightly off, and part of me still hears that voice, I guess.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. I mean, avoiding mistakes is part of it, but before I submit something I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll kind of mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, it’s not my entire day — I still get things done — but it makes me slower. And it takes some of the freedom out of the creative part, because instead of thinking, “This is good, send it,” I’m checking whether it feels settled enough. It’s funny, I’ll even get caught up on little things like alignment or crops, and then suddenly I’m remembering old design critiques, which is probably not even relevant anymore, but my brain goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel okay submitting your work if it was very good but not absolutely perfect?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yeah… I mean, “very good” should be enough, right? That’s what I’d tell someone else. But in the moment, my mind starts doing that loop where I think, what if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout, or what if I accidentally sent the wrong version to the client?\n\nAnd even when I can literally see it’s probably fine, I keep circling back to it. It reminds me of when I used to do print work and one little export setting could ruin everything—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I think I’d struggle a bit with “not absolutely perfect,” even if I knew it was good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeated checking is driven more by a fear of what might happen if you make a mistake, or by a desire to ensure that everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I need it to feel exactly as it should be. There is some fear of a mistake, sure, but it’s not like I’m imagining some disaster every time.\n\nIt’s more… before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then I might mentally walk through the design choices one more time. Which sounds so small when I say it, but it adds friction. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it makes me slower and less loose creatively.\n\nSometimes I miss when I could just look at something and think, “Okay, that works,” and send it. Now it has to feel settled first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is something you do to achieve a sense of personal satisfaction or perfection, rather than just to avoid mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s more like I need it to feel finished in the right way.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds… kind of excessive when I say it out loud. But in the moment, my mind starts doing that loop where I think, what if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout, or what if I accidentally sent the wrong version to the client?\n\nAnd even when I can literally see it’s probably fine, I keep circling back to it. It reminds me of when I used to do print work and one little export setting could ruin everything—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I think I’d struggle a bit with “not absolutely perfect,” even if I knew it was good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeated checking is driven more by a fear of what might happen if you make a mistake, or by a desire to ensure that everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I need it to feel exactly as it should be. There is some fear of a mistake, sure, but it’s not like I’m imagining some disaster every time.\n\nIt’s more… before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then I might mentally walk through the design choices one more time. Which sounds so small when I say it, but it adds friction. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it makes me slower and less loose creatively.\n\nSometimes I miss when I could just look at something and think, “Okay, that works,” and send it. Now it has to feel settled first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is something you do to achieve a sense of personal satisfaction or perfection, rather than just to avoid mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not only, “Oh no, I’ll make a mistake,” though that’s in there a little. It’s more like I need it to feel finished in the right way.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds… kind of excessive when I say it out loud. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does make me slower, and it makes the creative part feel less free.\n\nLike, instead of trusting my eye, I’m trying to get this little internal click of, okay, now it’s right.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your checking behavior is something you need to do to feel in control of the outcome?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / designer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"Before I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes mentally walk through every design choice one more time, which doesn’t take over my whole day but does make me slower and less free creatively.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think “control” is probably part of it. Not control in a huge dramatic way, but more like I need to feel I’ve pinned everything down before I let it go.

So before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll sort of replay the design choices in my head one more time. And it’s funny, because in design school they always talked about knowing when to stop, and I used to be better at that—sorry, that’s a bit of a tangent.

It doesn’t take over my whole day, exactly. But it does slow me down, and it makes the work feel less free, like I’m trying to control the outcome instead of just trusting that it’s good enough.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so I’m here because I keep getting these nagging thoughts that I’ve missed some tiny mistake in a layout, or that I sent the wrong version to a client. I’m a designer, so details matter, obviously, and clients can be really particular—like, sometimes they’ll notice a spacing thing you’d swear no one could see.\n\nBut even when I look and it seems fine, and I know it’s probably fine, my mind just keeps circling back to it. Like, “What if there’s something I missed?” or “What if that wasn’t the final file?” It’s not huge, I guess, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about potential mistakes come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… they do feel like they just pop in whether I want them to or not. I’ll have already looked at the layout and think, okay, that’s probably fine, and then my brain kind of loops back like, “What if there’s one tiny spacing mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?”\n\nIt’s annoying because with design you’re trained to notice small things—like kerning and alignment and all that, and I once had a client comment on something really microscopic—so part of me goes, well, maybe it’s reasonable. But it doesn’t really feel like I’m choosing to think about it. It just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do these recurring thoughts about potential mistakes cause you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I mean, “significant” sounds dramatic, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or anything. But it’s distressing in that it keeps pulling at me after I’ve already checked something and can see, logically, it’s probably fine.\n\nLike with a layout, I’ll think, okay, the spacing looks right, the file seems correct… and then I’m making coffee or answering another email and suddenly I’m back on, “What if I missed some tiny mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?” It’s this nagging feeling that won’t quite leave me alone. And then I end up annoyed with myself, because in design you’re supposed to be careful—but there’s careful, and then there’s my brain just circling the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work or taking specific actions to try and alleviate these nagging thoughts about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like, I don’t spend hours and hours on it, but before I submit something now I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it… sometimes more than once.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of mentally walk back through the whole design, like, “Why did I choose that spacing, is that aligned, does that crop make sense,” which—I mean, some of that is just part of being a designer. I had a professor in college who was obsessed with grids, actually, and I still hear her voice sometimes when I’m checking margins. But lately it feels less like normal review and more like I’m doing it to calm that nagging feeling.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it definitely slows me down. And I feel like it makes me less… loose creatively, I guess. Like I can’t just finish and move on.\n\nDoctor: Are you checking your work repeatedly because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, in a way. It’s not like I think something catastrophic is going to happen, exactly, but I’m trying to prevent that feeling of, “Oh no, I missed something,” or sending the wrong version and then looking careless.\n\nSo before I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds funny, because designers are supposed to review things—I mean, I had this one art director who would catch the tiniest alignment issues, like almost invisible—but with me lately it’s more like I’m doing it so I can feel safe enough to hit send.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just trust that it’s done and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked your work repeatedly?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of the annoying part—it does help for a minute. Like if I re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it, then I feel like, okay, I can send this now.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll still mentally go back through every choice—spacing, crop, alignment, all that. It’s funny, because in design school we were basically trained to be obsessive about details, and I used to think that was just professionalism. But now it feels more like I’m checking so I can quiet myself down.\n\nIt doesn’t eat up my whole day, but it definitely makes me slower. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I mean, sometimes I’ll remember some old critique from design school about spacing or crops, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of just submitting the thing. But no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down enough that I notice it, and I feel less loose creatively, if that makes sense. Like if I weren’t so tense about how it might land, I could probably let go sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your work repeatedly if you knew that making a small mistake would not have significant consequences?\n\nPatient: I think maybe the urge would still be there, just… less intense? Like even if someone told me, “A small mistake won’t matter,” I’d probably still want to re-open the file and compare the layers and re-save it, because that’s become this little ritual before I submit anything.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this thing where I mentally walk through the design choices again — which is annoying, because design is supposed to have some looseness to it. I remember in college we’d have these critiques where people would spend twenty minutes talking about one line being slightly off, and part of me still hears that voice, I guess.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. I mean, avoiding mistakes is part of it, but before I submit something I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll kind of mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, it’s not my entire day — I still get things done — but it makes me slower. And it takes some of the freedom out of the creative part, because instead of thinking, “This is good, send it,” I’m checking whether it feels settled enough. It’s funny, I’ll even get caught up on little things like alignment or crops, and then suddenly I’m remembering old design critiques, which is probably not even relevant anymore, but my brain goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel okay submitting your work if it was very good but not absolutely perfect?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yeah… I mean, “very good” should be enough, right? That’s what I’d tell someone else. But in the moment, my mind starts doing that loop where I think, what if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout, or what if I accidentally sent the wrong version to the client?\n\nAnd even when I can literally see it’s probably fine, I keep circling back to it. It reminds me of when I used to do print work and one little export setting could ruin everything—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I think I’d struggle a bit with “not absolutely perfect,” even if I knew it was good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeated checking is driven more by a fear of what might happen if you make a mistake, or by a desire to ensure that everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I need it to feel exactly as it should be. There is some fear of a mistake, sure, but it’s not like I’m imagining some disaster every time.\n\nIt’s more… before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then I might mentally walk through the design choices one more time. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it makes me slower, and I feel less free creatively because I’m sort of trying to make the work feel safe before I send it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is more about making sure everything is perfect, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think “perfect” is closer, though I don’t know if I mean perfect in some grand way. It’s more like I can’t quite leave it alone until it feels… settled.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Like, did I mean to align it that way, is that crop too tight, that kind of thing. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I mean, sometimes I’ll remember some old critique from design school about spacing or crops, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of just submitting the thing. But no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down enough that I notice it, and I feel less loose creatively, if that makes sense. Like if I weren’t so tense about how it might land, I could probably let go sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your work repeatedly if you knew that making a small mistake would not have significant consequences?\n\nPatient: I think maybe the urge would still be there, just… less intense? I remember in college we’d have these critiques where people would spend twenty minutes talking about one line being slightly off, and part of me still hears that voice, I guess.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop. It’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll tell myself, okay, just send the file, and then I’m back in there reopening it, comparing the layers, saving it again, then kind of mentally replaying the design choices one more time.\n\nIt’s a little ridiculous because half the time nothing changes. I mean, sometimes I’ll remember some old critique from design school about spacing or crops, and then I’m off thinking about that instead of just submitting the thing. But no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down enough that I notice it, and I feel less loose creatively, if that makes sense. Like if I weren’t so tense about how it might land, I could probably let go sooner.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your work repeatedly if you knew that making a small mistake would not have significant consequences?\n\nPatient: I think maybe the urge would still be there, just… less intense? Like even if someone told me, “A small mistake won’t matter,” I’d probably still want to re-open the file and compare the layers and re-save it, because that’s become this little ritual before I submit anything.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this thing where I mentally walk through the design choices again — which is annoying, because design is supposed to have some looseness to it. I remember in college we’d have these critiques where people would spend twenty minutes talking about one line being slightly off, and part of me still hears that voice, I guess.\n\nBut no, it doesn’t take over my whole day. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. I mean, avoiding mistakes is part of it, but before I submit something I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll kind of mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, it’s not my entire day — I still get things done — but it makes me slower. And it takes some of the freedom out of the creative part, because instead of thinking, “This is good, send it,” I’m checking whether it feels settled enough. It’s funny, I’ll even get caught up on little things like alignment or crops, and then suddenly I’m remembering old design critiques, which is probably not even relevant anymore, but my brain goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel okay submitting your work if it was very good but not absolutely perfect?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yeah… I mean, “very good” should be enough, right? That’s what I’d tell someone else. But in the moment, my mind starts doing that loop where I think, what if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout, or what if I accidentally sent the wrong version to the client?\n\nAnd even when I can literally see it’s probably fine, I keep circling back to it. It reminds me of when I used to do print work and one little export setting could ruin everything—sorry, that’s probably not the point. But yes, I think I’d struggle a bit with “not absolutely perfect,” even if I knew it was good.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repeated checking is driven more by a fear of what might happen if you make a mistake, or by a desire to ensure that everything is exactly as it should be?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I need it to feel exactly as it should be. There is some fear of a mistake, sure, but it’s not like I’m imagining some disaster every time.\n\nIt’s more… before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then I might mentally walk through the design choices one more time. Which sounds so small when I say it, but it adds friction. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it makes me slower and less loose creatively.\n\nSometimes I miss when I could just look at something and think, “Okay, that works,” and send it. Now it has to feel settled first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is something you do to achieve a sense of personal satisfaction or perfection, rather than just to avoid mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not only, “Oh no, I’ll make a mistake,” though that’s in there a little. It’s more like I need it to feel finished in the right way.\n\nBefore I submit work now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes mentally walk through every design choice one more time. Which sounds… kind of excessive when I say it out loud. It doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does make me slower, and it makes the creative part feel less free.\n\nLike, instead of trusting my eye, I’m trying to get this little internal click of, okay, now it’s right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behavior is something you need to do to feel in control of the outcome?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “control” is probably part of it. Not control in a huge dramatic way, but more like I need to feel I’ve pinned everything down before I let it go.\n\nSo before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll sort of replay the design choices in my head one more time. And it’s funny, because in design school they always talked about knowing when to stop, and I used to be better at that—sorry, that’s a bit of a tangent.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, exactly. But it does slow me down, and it makes the work feel less free, like I’m trying to control the outcome instead of just trusting that it’s good enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still check your work repeatedly if you were certain that no one would notice any small mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… probably yes, honestly.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think maybe, yes… if I could really accept “good enough,” I probably wouldn’t need to do it as much. That’s kind of the whole snag, I guess.

Because what happens now is, before I submit something, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and sometimes walk through every design choice in my head one more time. And it’s not like it eats up my whole day, but it does make me slower. It also makes the creative part feel less… loose, less free.

It reminds me a little of when I’m arranging things on a mood board and I keep moving one tiny piece back and forth—sorry, that’s not exactly what you asked. But yes, if I could actually feel that it was good enough without needing that last check, I think I could stop sooner.

But it’s distressing in that it keeps pulling at me after I’ve already checked something and can see, logically, it’s probably fine.\n\nLike with a layout, I’ll think, okay, the spacing looks right, the file seems correct… and then I’m making coffee or answering another email and suddenly I’m back on, “What if I missed some tiny mistake?” or “What if I sent the wrong version?” It’s this nagging feeling that won’t quite leave me alone. And then I end up annoyed with myself, because in design you’re supposed to be careful—but there’s careful, and then there’s my brain just circling the same thing over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work or taking specific actions to try and alleviate these nagging thoughts about potential mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. It’s more like I can’t quite let the work leave my hands until I feel like I’ve pinned everything down.\n\nSo before I submit something now, I’ll re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it, and then sometimes I’ll mentally walk through the design choices again — like whether the crop was intentional or if the spacing feels right. Which is funny because in design school they were always so dramatic about tiny details, and I used to love that part, like the craft of it.\n\nBut now it’s less satisfying. It just slows me down and makes me feel less free creatively, like I can’t just send the work and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to repeatedly check your work is more about avoiding mistakes or making sure everything is just right?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the “just right” thing, honestly. And it takes some of the freedom out of the creative part, because instead of thinking, “This is good, send it,” I’m checking whether it feels settled enough. It’s funny, I’ll even get caught up on little things like alignment or crops, and then suddenly I’m remembering old design critiques, which is probably not even relevant anymore, but my brain goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel okay submitting your work if it was very good but not absolutely perfect?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think so, yeah… I mean, “very good” should be enough, right? That’s what I’d tell someone else. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just trust that it’s done and move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked your work repeatedly?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, that’s kind of the annoying part—it does help for a minute. Like if I re-open the file, compare the layers, make sure it’s the right version, re-save it, then I feel like, okay, I can send this now.\n\nBut then sometimes I’ll still mentally go back through every choice—spacing, crop, alignment, all that. It’s funny, because in design school we were basically trained to be obsessive about details, and I used to think that was just professionalism. But now it feels more like I’m checking so I can quiet myself down.\n\nIt doesn’t eat up my whole day, but it definitely makes me slower. And I feel less free creatively, like I can’t just finish something and trust it’s done.\n\nDoctor: Are you doing these checks to reduce your anxiety about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s mostly why I do it. Before I submit something, I’ll reopen the file, compare the layers, re-save it… sometimes I’ll do that whole little routine again even if I already know it’s probably fine.\n\nAnd then I’ll mentally go through the design choices one more time, like, “Was that crop intentional, is the spacing okay, did I use the right version.” It’s funny, my old studio manager used to say designers should always “measure twice, send once,” which sounded clever at the time, but now I think I took that a little too literally.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it slows me down. And it makes me feel less loose creatively, like I’m not just finishing the work — I’m trying to make the anxious feeling settle enough that I can let it go.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeated checks primarily about preventing something specific from going wrong, or is it more about just feeling less anxious in general?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about feeling less anxious, honestly. I mean, there isn’t usually one specific disaster I’m picturing. It’s more like before I submit something, I get this uncomfortable sense that I might have missed something, so I re-open the file, compare the layers, re-save it… and sometimes I’ll mentally walk through every design choice again.\n\nLike, “Is that spacing okay, did I mean to crop it that way,” that kind of thing. Which, you know, in design some checking is normal—my professors used to drill that into us, and I still hear one of them saying, “Don’t let a sloppy file represent you,” which is maybe not helpful now. But lately it’s less about a specific thing going wrong and more about needing to feel settled enough to send it.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day, but it does slow me down, and I feel less free creatively because I can’t just trust that it’s finished.\n\nDoctor: Are these checks affecting your ability to meet deadlines or complete tasks on time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m still getting the work done, so it’s not like I’m completely failing to function or anything. But I have started missing some internal deadlines, like the softer ones before the actual client deadline, because I keep holding things back to check them again.\n\nAnd I guess there’s another part of it too — I’ve been playing it safer with design ideas. Which is frustrating, because I’ve always wanted to move more toward a creative director role, and you kind of have to show you can take bigger swings. But then I think, what if I overlook something, or the file has some stupid mistake, and that hurts how people see me?\n\nSorry, I’m kind of rambling. The short answer is yes, a bit. I’m still completing tasks, but it’s slowing me down and making me less bold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking your work repeatedly because you need to have control over the outcome, rather than just preventing mistakes?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe a little, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as controlling the whole outcome exactly. And then I’ll remember some professor from school making a huge deal about craftsmanship, which probably stuck with me more than it should have.\n\nIt doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it definitely makes me slower. And creatively, I feel less free, because instead of just sending something I’m proud of, I’m trying to make sure it’s sort of… perfectly safe first.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you could stop checking your work if you felt less anxious about the potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes… or at least I think it would be easier to stop.
